The warehouse becomes the central source of data for use by managers and other end-users who may not have access to operational data. For example, sales data might be aggregated to weekly totals and converted from internal product codes to use <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Product_Code" title="Universal Product Code" id="mwAcE">UPCs</a> so that they can be compared with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACNielsen" title="ACNielsen" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAcI">ACNielsen</a> data. Some basic and essential components of data warehousing include extracting, analyzing, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_mining" title="Data mining" id="mwAcM">mining</a> data, transforming, loading, and managing data so as to make them available for further use.</li>
<li id="mwAcQ">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_database" title="Deductive database" id="mwAcU">deductive database</a> combines <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_programming" title="Logic programming" id="mwAcY">logic programming</a> with a relational database.</li>
<li id="mwAcc">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distributed_database" title="Distributed database" id="mwAcg">distributed database</a> is one in which both the data and the DBMS span multiple computers.</li>
<li id="mwAck">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Document-oriented_database" title="Document-oriented database" id="mwAco">document-oriented database</a> is designed for storing, retrieving, and managing document-oriented, or semi structured, information. Document-oriented databases are one of the main categories of NoSQL databases.</li>
<li id="mwAcs">An <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embedded_database" title="Embedded database" id="mwAcw">embedded database</a> system is a DBMS which is tightly integrated with an application software that requires access to stored data in such a way that the DBMS is hidden from the application's end-users and requires little or no ongoing maintenance.<sup about="#mwt115" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-26" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-26"}}'><a href="#cite_note-26" id="mwAc0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAc4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAc8">[</span>26<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdA">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwAdE">End-user databases consist of data developed by individual end-users. Examples of these are collections of documents, spreadsheets, presentations, multimedia, and other files. Several products<link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:All_articles_with_specifically_marked_weasel-worded_phrases" about="#mwt116" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAdI" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"which","href":"./Template:Which"},"params":{"date":{"wt":"December 2022"}},"i":0}}]}'/><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_with_specifically_marked_weasel-worded_phrases_from_December_2022" about="#mwt116"/><sup class="noprint Inline-Template " style="white-space:nowrap;" about="#mwt116" id="mwAdM"><span typeof="mw:Entity">[</span><i><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Avoid_weasel_words" title="Wikipedia:Avoid weasel words" class="mw-redirect"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too vague attribution or weasel words. (December 2022)">which?</span></a></i><span typeof="mw:Entity">]</span></sup> exist to support such databases.</li>
<li id="mwAdQ">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_database_system" title="Federated database system" id="mwAdU">federated database system</a> comprises several distinct databases, each with its own DBMS. It is handled as a single database by a federated database management system (FDBMS), which transparently integrates multiple autonomous DBMSs, possibly of different types (in which case it would also be a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterogeneous_database_system" title="Heterogeneous database system" id="mwAdY">heterogeneous database system</a>), and provides them with an integrated conceptual view.</li>
<li id="mwAdc">Sometimes the term <i id="mwAdg">multi-database</i> is used as a synonym for federated database, though it may refer to a less integrated (e.g., without an FDBMS and a managed integrated schema) group of databases that cooperate in a single application. In this case, typically <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middleware_(distributed_applications)" title="Middleware (distributed applications)" id="mwAdk">middleware</a> is used for distribution, which typically includes an atomic commit protocol (ACP), e.g., the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-phase_commit_protocol" title="Two-phase commit protocol" id="mwAdo">two-phase commit protocol</a>, to allow <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distributed_transaction" title="Distributed transaction" id="mwAds">distributed (global) transactions</a> across the participating databases.</li>
<li id="mwAdw">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graph_database" title="Graph database" id="mwAd0">graph database</a> is a kind of NoSQL database that uses <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graph_(data_structure)" title="Graph (data structure)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAd4">graph structures</a> with nodes, edges, and properties to represent and store information. General graph databases that can store any graph are distinct from specialized graph databases such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triplestore" title="Triplestore" id="mwAd8">triplestores</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_database_model" title="Network database model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAeA">network databases</a>.</li>
<li id="mwAeE">An <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Array_DBMS" title="Array DBMS" id="mwAeI">array DBMS</a> is a kind of NoSQL DBMS that allows modeling, storage, and retrieval of (usually large) multi-dimensional <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Array_data_structure" title="Array data structure" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAeM">arrays</a> such as satellite images and climate simulation output.</li>
<li id="mwAeQ">In a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypertext" title="Hypertext" id="mwAeU">hypertext</a> or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypermedia" title="Hypermedia" id="mwAeY">hypermedia</a> database, any word or a piece of text representing an object, e.g., another piece of text, an article, a picture, or a film, can be <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperlink" title="Hyperlink" id="mwAec">hyperlinked</a> to that object. Hypertext databases are particularly useful for organizing large amounts of disparate information. For example, they are useful for organizing <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_encyclopedia" title="Online encyclopedia" id="mwAeg">online encyclopedias</a>, where users can conveniently jump around the text. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web" title="World Wide Web" id="mwAek">World Wide Web</a> is thus a large distributed hypertext database.</li>
<li id="mwAeo">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_base" title="Knowledge base" id="mwAes">knowledge base</a> (abbreviated <b id="mwAew">KB</b>, <b id="mwAe0">kb</b> or Δ<sup about="#mwt117" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-27" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-27"}}'><a href="#cite_note-27" id="mwAe4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAe8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAfA">[</span>27<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAfE">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt120" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-28" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-28"}}'><a href="#cite_note-28" id="mwAfI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAfM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAfQ">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAfU">]</span></span></a></sup>) is a special kind of database for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_management" title="Knowledge management" id="mwAfY">knowledge management</a>, providing the means for the computerized collection, organization, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_retrieval" title="Information retrieval" id="mwAfc">retrieval</a> of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge" title="Knowledge" id="mwAfg">knowledge</a>. Also a collection of data representing problems with their solutions and related experiences.</li></ul>

<ul id="mwAfk"><li id="mwAfo">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_database" title="Mobile database" id="mwAfs">mobile database</a> can be carried on or synchronized from a mobile computing device.</li>
<li id="mwAfw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operational_database" title="Operational database" id="mwAf0">Operational databases</a> store detailed data about the operations of an organization. They typically process relatively high volumes of updates using <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transaction_(database)" title="Transaction (database)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAf4">transactions</a>. Examples include <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customer_relationship_management" title="Customer relationship management" id="mwAf8">customer databases</a> that record contact, credit, and demographic information about a business's customers, personnel databases that hold information such as salary, benefits, skills data about employees, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enterprise_resource_planning" title="Enterprise resource planning" id="mwAgA">enterprise resource planning</a> systems that record details about product components, parts inventory, and financial databases that keep track of the organization's money, accounting and financial dealings.</li>
<li id="mwAgE">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_database" title="Parallel database" id="mwAgI">parallel database</a> seeks to improve performance through <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallelization" title="Parallelization" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAgM">parallelization</a> for tasks such as loading data, building indexes and evaluating queries.</li></ul>

<dl id="mwAgQ"><dd id="mwAgU"><dl id="mwAgY"><dd id="mwAgc">The major parallel DBMS architectures which are induced by the underlying <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_hardware" title="Computer hardware" id="mwAgg">hardware</a> architecture are:
<ul id="mwAgk"><li id="mwAgo"><b id="mwAgs"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared_memory_architecture" title="Shared memory architecture" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAgw">Shared memory architecture</a></b>, where multiple processors share the main memory space, as well as other data storage.</li>
<li id="mwAg0"><b id="mwAg4">Shared disk architecture</b>, where each processing unit (typically consisting of multiple processors) has its own main memory, but all units share the other storage.</li>
<li id="mwAg8"><b id="mwAhA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared-nothing_architecture" title="Shared-nothing architecture" id="mwAhE">Shared-nothing architecture</a></b>, where each processing unit has its own main memory and other storage.</li></ul></dd></dl></dd></dl>

<ul id="mwAhI"><li id="mwAhM"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probabilistic_database" title="Probabilistic database" id="mwAhQ">Probabilistic databases</a> employ <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzzy_logic" title="Fuzzy logic" id="mwAhU">fuzzy logic</a> to draw inferences from imprecise data.</li>
<li id="mwAhY"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real-time_database" title="Real-time database" id="mwAhc">Real-time databases</a> process transactions fast enough for the result to come back and be acted on right away.</li>
<li id="mwAhg">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_database" title="Spatial database" id="mwAhk">spatial database</a> can store the data with multidimensional features. The queries on such data include location-based queries, like "Where is the closest hotel in my area?".</li>
<li id="mwAho">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporal_database" title="Temporal database" id="mwAhs">temporal database</a> has built-in time aspects, for example a temporal data model and a temporal version of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SQL" title="SQL" id="mwAhw">SQL</a>. More specifically the temporal aspects usually include valid-time and transaction-time.</li>
<li id="mwAh0">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminology-oriented_database" title="Terminology-oriented database" id="mwAh4">terminology-oriented database</a> builds upon an <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Object-oriented_database" title="Object-oriented database" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAh8">object-oriented database</a>, often customized for a specific field.</li>
<li id="mwAiA">An <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unstructured_data" title="Unstructured data" id="mwAiE">unstructured data</a> database is intended to store in a manageable and protected way diverse objects that do not fit naturally and conveniently in common databases. It may include email messages, documents, journals, multimedia objects, etc. The name may be misleading since some objects can be highly structured. However, the entire possible object collection does not fit into a predefined structured framework. Most established DBMSs now support unstructured data in various ways, and new dedicated DBMSs are emerging.<!-- Isn't this a document&#x2D;oriented database? If not, clearly distinguish. --></li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="12" id="mwAiI" aria-labelledby="Database_management_system"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Database_management_system">Database management system</h2></div>

<p id="mwAiM">Connolly and Begg define database management system (DBMS) as a "software system that enables users to define, create, maintain and control access to the database."<sup about="#mwt121" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201464_29-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201464"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201464-29"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Connolly"},"2":{"wt":"Begg"},"3":{"wt":"2014"},"p":{"wt":"64"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201464-29" id="mwAiQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAiU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAiY">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAic">]</span></span></a></sup> Examples of DBMS include <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MySQL" title="MySQL" id="mwAig">MySQL</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MariaDB" title="MariaDB" id="mwAik">MariaDB</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PostgreSQL" title="PostgreSQL" id="mwAio">PostgreSQL</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_SQL_Server" title="Microsoft SQL Server" id="mwAis">Microsoft SQL Server</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oracle_Database" title="Oracle Database" id="mwAiw">Oracle Database</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_Access" title="Microsoft Access" id="mwAi0">Microsoft Access</a>.</p>

<p id="mwAi4">The DBMS acronym is sometimes extended to indicate the underlying <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_model" title="Database model" id="mwAi8">database model</a>, with RDBMS for the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relational_model" title="Relational model" id="mwAjA">relational</a>, OODBMS for the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Object_model" title="Object model" id="mwAjE">object (oriented)</a> and ORDBMS for the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Object–relational_model" title="Object–relational model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAjI">object–relational model</a>. Other extensions can indicate some other characteristics, such as DDBMS for a distributed database management systems.</p>

<p id="mwAjM">The functionality provided by a DBMS can vary enormously. The core functionality is the storage, retrieval and update of data. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_F._Codd" title="Edgar F. Codd" id="mwAjQ">Codd</a> proposed the following functions and services a fully-fledged general purpose DBMS should provide:<sup about="#mwt124" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201497–102_30-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201497–102"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201497–102-30"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Connolly"},"2":{"wt":"Begg"},"3":{"wt":"2014"},"pp":{"wt":"97–102"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201497–102-30" id="mwAjU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjc">[</span>30<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjg">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<ul id="mwAjk"><li id="mwAjo">Data storage, retrieval and update</li>
<li id="mwAjs">User accessible catalog or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_dictionary" title="Data dictionary" id="mwAjw">data dictionary</a> describing the metadata</li>
<li id="mwAj0">Support for transactions and concurrency</li>
<li id="mwAj4">Facilities for recovering the database should it become damaged</li>
<li id="mwAj8">Support for authorization of access and update of data</li>
<li id="mwAkA">Access support from remote locations</li>
<li id="mwAkE">Enforcing constraints to ensure data in the database abides by certain rules</li></ul>

<p id="mwAkI">It is also generally to be expected the DBMS will provide a set of utilities for such purposes as may be necessary to administer the database effectively, including import, export, monitoring, defragmentation and analysis utilities.<sup about="#mwt127" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014102_31-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014102"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014102-31"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Connolly"},"2":{"wt":"Begg"},"3":{"wt":"2014"},"p":{"wt":"102"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014102-31" id="mwAkM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAkQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkU">[</span>31<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkY">]</span></span></a></sup> The core part of the DBMS interacting between the database and the application interface sometimes referred to as the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_engine" title="Database engine" id="mwAkc">database engine</a>.</p>

<p id="mwAkg">Often DBMSs will have configuration parameters that can be statically and dynamically tuned, for example the maximum amount of main memory on a server the database can use. The trend is to minimize the amount of manual configuration, and for cases such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embedded_database" title="Embedded database" id="mwAkk">embedded databases</a> the need to target zero-administration is paramount.</p>

<p id="mwAko">The large major enterprise DBMSs have tended to increase in size and functionality and have involved up to thousands of human and years of development effort throughout their lifetime.<sup about="#mwt130" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-33" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"lower-alpha"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-33"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"efn","href":"./Template:Efn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"This article quotes a development time of five years involving 750 people for DB2 release 9 alone.{{sfn|Chong|Wang|Dang|Snow|2007}}"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-33" data-mw-group="lower-alpha" id="mwAks"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAkw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAk0">[</span>a<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAk4">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAk8">Early multi-user DBMS typically only allowed for the application to reside on the same computer with access via <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_terminal" title="Computer terminal" id="mwAlA">terminals</a> or terminal emulation software. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Client–server_architecture" title="Client–server architecture" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAlE">client–server architecture</a> was a development where the application resided on a client desktop and the database on a server allowing the processing to be distributed. This evolved into a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multitier_architecture" title="Multitier architecture" id="mwAlI">multitier architecture</a> incorporating <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Application_server" title="Application server" id="mwAlM">application servers</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_server" title="Web server" id="mwAlQ">web servers</a> with the end user interface via a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_browser" title="Web browser" id="mwAlU">web browser</a> with the database only directly connected to the adjacent tier.<sup about="#mwt136" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014106–113_34-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014106–113"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014106–113-34"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Connolly"},"2":{"wt":"Begg"},"3":{"wt":"2014"},"pp":{"wt":"106–113"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg2014106–113-34" id="mwAlY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAlc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAlg">[</span>33<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAlk">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAlo">A general-purpose DBMS will provide public <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Application_programming_interface" title="Application programming interface" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAls">application programming interfaces</a> (API) and optionally a processor for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_language" title="Database language" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAlw">database languages</a> such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SQL" title="SQL" id="mwAl0">SQL</a> to allow applications to be written to interact with and manipulate the database. A special purpose DBMS may use a private API and be specifically customized and linked to a single application. For example, an <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Email" title="Email" id="mwAl4">email</a> system performs many of the functions of a general-purpose DBMS such as message insertion, message deletion, attachment handling, blocklist lookup, associating messages an email address and so forth however these functions are limited to what is required to handle email.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="13" id="mwAl8" aria-labelledby="Application"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Application">Application</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt139" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAmA" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Database application"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt140" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt139" id="mwAmE">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_application" title="Database application">Database application</a></div>
<p id="mwAmI">External interaction with the database will be via an application program that interfaces with the DBMS.<sup about="#mwt141" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201465_35-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201465"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201465-35"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Connolly"},"2":{"wt":"Begg"},"3":{"wt":"2014"},"p":{"wt":"65"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnollyBegg201465-35" id="mwAmM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAmQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmU">[</span>34<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmY">]</span></span></a></sup> This can range from a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparison_of_database_tools" title="Comparison of database tools" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAmc">database tool</a> that allows users to execute SQL queries textually or graphically, to a website that happens to use a database to store and search information.</p>

<section data-mw-section-id="14" id="mwAmg" aria-labelledby="Application_program_interface"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Application_program_interface">Application program interface</h3></div>

<p id="mwAmk">A <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programmer" title="Programmer" id="mwAmo">programmer</a> will <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_programming" title="Computer programming" id="mwAms">code</a> interactions to the database (sometimes referred to as a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Datasource" title="Datasource" id="mwAmw">datasource</a>) via an <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Application_program_interface" title="Application program interface" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAm0">application program interface</a> (API) or via a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="#database_language" class="mw-selflink-fragment" id="mwAm4">database language</a>. The particular API or language chosen will need to be supported by DBMS, possibly indirectly via a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preprocessor" title="Preprocessor" id="mwAm8">preprocessor</a> or a bridging API. Some API's aim to be database independent, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ODBC" title="ODBC" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAnA">ODBC</a> being a commonly known example. Other common API's include <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JDBC" title="JDBC" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAnE">JDBC</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADO.NET" title="ADO.NET" id="mwAnI">ADO.NET</a>.</p>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="15" id="mwAnM" aria-labelledby="Database_languages"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Database_languages">Database languages</h2></div>

<p id="mwAnQ">Database languages are special-purpose languages, which allow one or more of the following tasks, sometimes distinguished as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublanguage" title="Sublanguage" id="mwAnU">sublanguages</a>:</p>

<ul id="mwAnY"><li id="mwAnc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_control_language" title="Data control language" id="mwAng">Data control language</a> (DCL) – controls access to data;</li>
<li id="mwAnk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_definition_language" title="Data definition language" id="mwAno">Data definition language</a> (DDL) – defines data types such as creating, altering, or dropping tables and the relationships among them;</li>
<li id="mwAns"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_manipulation_language" title="Data manipulation language" id="mwAnw">Data manipulation language</a> (DML) – performs tasks such as inserting, updating, or deleting data occurrences;</li>
<li id="mwAn0"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_query_language" title="Data query language" id="mwAn4">Data query language</a> (DQL) – allows searching for information and computing derived information.</li></ul>

<p id="mwAn8">Database languages are specific to a particular data model. Notable examples include:</p>

<ul id="mwAoA"><li id="mwAoE">SQL combines the roles of data definition, data manipulation, and query in a single language. It was one of the first commercial languages for the relational model, although it departs in some respects from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codd's_12_rules" title="Codd's 12 rules" id="mwAoI">the relational model as described by Codd</a> (for example, the rows and columns of a table can be ordered). SQL became a standard of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_National_Standards_Institute" title="American National Standards Institute" id="mwAoM">American National Standards Institute</a> (ANSI) in 1986, and of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Organization_for_Standardization" title="International Organization for Standardization" id="mwAoQ">International Organization for Standardization</a> (ISO) in 1987. The standards have been regularly enhanced since and are supported (with varying degrees of conformance) by all mainstream commercial relational DBMSs.<sup about="#mwt144" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChapple2005_36-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEChapple2005"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEChapple2005-36"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Chapple"},"2":{"wt":"2005"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChapple2005-36" id="mwAoU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAoY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAoc">[</span>35<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAog">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt149" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-IBM-sql_37-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"IBM-sql"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-IBM-sql-37"}}'><a href="#cite_note-IBM-sql-37" id="mwAok"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAoo"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAos">[</span>36<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAow">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwAo0"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OQL" title="OQL" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAo4">OQL</a> is an object model language standard (from the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Object_Data_Management_Group" title="Object Data Management Group" id="mwAo8">Object Data Management Group</a>). It has influenced the design of some of the newer query languages like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JDOQL?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="JDOQL (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwApA" data-mw-i18n='{"title":{"lang":"x-page","key":"red-link-title","params":["JDOQL"]}}'>JDOQL</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EJB_QL" title="EJB QL" id="mwApE">EJB QL</a>.</li>
<li id="mwApI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XQuery" title="XQuery" id="mwApM">XQuery</a> is a standard XML query language implemented by XML database systems such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MarkLogic" title="MarkLogic" id="mwApQ">MarkLogic</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EXist" title="EXist" id="mwApU">eXist</a>, by relational databases with XML capability such as Oracle and Db2, and also by in-memory XML processors such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxon_XSLT" title="Saxon XSLT" id="mwApY">Saxon</a>.</li>
<li id="mwApc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SQL/XML" title="SQL/XML" id="mwApg">SQL/XML</a> combines <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XQuery" title="XQuery" id="mwApk">XQuery</a> with SQL.<sup about="#mwt150" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWagner2010_38-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEWagner2010"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagner2010-38"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Wagner"},"2":{"wt":"2010"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWagner2010-38" id="mwApo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAps"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApw">[</span>37<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAp0">]</span></span></a></sup></li></ul>

<p id="mwAp4">A database language may also incorporate features like:</p>

<ul id="mwAp8"><li id="mwAqA">DBMS-specific configuration and storage engine management</li>
<li id="mwAqE">Computations to modify query results, like counting, summing, averaging, sorting, grouping, and cross-referencing</li>
<li id="mwAqI">Constraint enforcement (e.g. in an automotive database, only allowing one engine type per car)</li>
<li id="mwAqM">Application programming interface version of the query language, for programmer convenience</li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="16" id="mwAqQ" aria-labelledby="Storage"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Storage">Storage</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt153" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAqU" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Computer data storage"},"2":{"wt":"Database engine"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt154" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt153" id="mwAqY">Main articles: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_data_storage" title="Computer data storage">Computer data storage</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_engine" title="Database engine">Database engine</a></div>

<p id="mwAqc">Database storage is the container of the physical materialization of a database. It comprises the <i id="mwAqg">internal</i> (physical) <i id="mwAqk">level</i> in the database architecture. It also contains all the information needed (e.g., <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metadata" title="Metadata" id="mwAqo">metadata</a>, "data about the data", and internal <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_structure" title="Data structure" id="mwAqs">data structures</a>) to reconstruct the <i id="mwAqw">conceptual level</i> and <i id="mwAq0">external level</i> from the internal level when needed. Databases as digital objects contain three layers of information which must be stored: the data, the structure, and the semantics. Proper storage of all three layers is needed for future <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_preservation" title="Database preservation" id="mwAq4">preservation</a> and longevity of the database.<sup about="#mwt157" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-39" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-39"}}'><a href="#cite_note-39" id="mwAq8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArE">[</span>38<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArI">]</span></span></a></sup> Putting data into permanent storage is generally the responsibility of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_engine" title="Database engine" id="mwArM">database engine</a> a.k.a. "storage engine". Though typically accessed by a DBMS through the underlying operating system (and often using the operating systems' <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File_system" title="File system" id="mwArQ">file systems</a> as intermediates for storage layout), storage properties and configuration settings are extremely important for the efficient operation of the DBMS, and thus are closely maintained by database administrators. A DBMS, while in operation, always has its database residing in several types of storage (e.g., memory and external storage). The database data and the additional needed information, possibly in very large amounts, are coded into bits. Data typically reside in the storage in structures that look completely different from the way the data look at the conceptual and external levels, but in ways that attempt to optimize (the best possible) these levels' reconstruction when needed by users and programs, as well as for computing additional types of needed information from the data (e.g., when querying the database).</p>

<p id="mwArU">Some DBMSs support specifying which <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Character_encoding" title="Character encoding" id="mwArY">character encoding</a> was used to store data, so multiple encodings can be used in the same database.</p>

<p id="mwArc">Various low-level database storage structures are used by the storage engine to serialize the data model so it can be written to the medium of choice. Techniques such as indexing may be used to improve performance. Conventional storage is row-oriented, but there are also <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Column-oriented_DBMS" title="Column-oriented DBMS" class="mw-redirect" id="mwArg">column-oriented</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correlation_database?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Correlation database (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwArk" data-mw-i18n='{"title":{"lang":"x-page","key":"red-link-title","params":["Correlation database"]}}'>correlation databases</a>.</p>

<section data-mw-section-id="17" id="mwAro" aria-labelledby="Materialized_views"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Materialized_views">Materialized views</h3></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt158" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwArs" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Materialized view"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt159" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt158" id="mwArw">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialized_view" title="Materialized view">Materialized view</a></div>

<p id="mwAr0">Often storage redundancy is employed to increase performance. A common example is storing <i id="mwAr4">materialized views</i>, which consist of frequently needed <i id="mwAr8">external views</i> or query results. Storing such views saves the expensive computing them each time they are needed. The downsides of materialized views are the overhead incurred when updating them to keep them synchronized with their original updated database data, and the cost of storage redundancy.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="18" id="mwAsA" aria-labelledby="Replication"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Replication">Replication</h3></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt160" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAsE" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"See also","href":"./Template:See_also"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Replication (computing)#Database replication"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt161" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt160" id="mwAsI">See also: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replication_(computing)#Database_replication" title="Replication (computing)">Replication (computing) §<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>Database replication</a></div>

<p id="mwAsM">Occasionally a database employs storage redundancy by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_object" title="Database object" id="mwAsQ">database objects</a> replication (with one or more copies) to increase data availability (both to improve performance of simultaneous multiple end-user accesses to the same database object, and to provide resiliency in a case of partial failure of a distributed database). Updates of a replicated object need to be synchronized across the object copies. In many cases, the entire database is replicated.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="19" id="mwAsU" aria-labelledby="Virtualization"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Virtualization">Virtualization</h3></div>
<p id="mwAsY">With <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_virtualization" title="Data virtualization" id="mwAsc">data virtualization</a>, the data used remains in its original locations and real-time access is established to allow analytics across multiple sources. This can aid in resolving some technical difficulties such as compatibility problems when combining data from various platforms, lowering the risk of error caused by faulty data, and guaranteeing that the newest data is used. Furthermore, avoiding the creation of a new database containing personal information can make it easier to comply with privacy regulations. However, with data virtualization, the connection to all necessary data sources must be operational as there is no local copy of the data, which is one of the main drawbacks of the approach.<sup about="#mwt164" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Paiho_40-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"Paiho"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-Paiho-40"}}'><a href="#cite_note-Paiho-40" id="mwAsg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAsk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAso">[</span>39<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAss">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="20" id="mwAsw" aria-labelledby="Security"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Security">Security</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt165" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAs0" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Database security"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt166" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt165" id="mwAs4">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_security" title="Database security" class="mw-redirect">Database security</a></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt167" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAs8" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Contradicts other","href":"./Template:Contradicts_other"},"params":{"date":{"wt":"March 2013"},"1":{"wt":"Database security"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1364180890" about="#mwt168" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Message box/ambox.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><table class="box-Contradicts_others plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content" role="presentation" about="#mwt167"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ambox_contradict.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Ambox_contradict.svg/40px-Ambox_contradict.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Ambox_contradict.svg/120px-Ambox_contradict.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" alt="" data-file-width="620" data-file-height="620" data-file-type="drawing" height="40" width="40" class="mw-file-element"/></span></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This article <b>appears to contradict the article <i><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_security" title="Database security" class="mw-redirect">Database security</a></i></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please discuss at the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Database" title="Talk:Database">talk page</a> and do not remove this message until the contradictions are resolved.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">March 2013</span>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt167"><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_contradicting_other_articles"/></span>

<p id="mwAtA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_security" title="Database security" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAtE">Database security</a> deals with all various aspects of protecting the database content, its owners, and its users. It ranges from protection from intentional unauthorized database uses to unintentional database accesses by unauthorized entities (e.g., a person or a computer program).</p>

<p id="mwAtI">Database access control deals with controlling who (a person or a certain computer program) are allowed to access what information in the database. The information may comprise specific database objects (e.g., record types, specific records, data structures), certain computations over certain objects (e.g., query types, or specific queries), or using specific access paths to the former (e.g., using specific indexes or other data structures to access information). Database access controls are set by special authorized (by the database owner) personnel that uses dedicated protected security DBMS interfaces.</p>

<p id="mwAtM">This may be managed directly on an individual basis, or by the assignment of individuals and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privilege_(Computing)" title="Privilege (Computing)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAtQ">privileges</a> to groups, or (in the most elaborate models) through the assignment of individuals and groups to roles which are then granted entitlements. Data security prevents unauthorized users from viewing or updating the database. Using passwords, users are allowed access to the entire database or subsets of it called "subschemas". For example, an employee database can contain all the data about an individual employee, but one group of users may be authorized to view only payroll data, while others are allowed access to only work history and medical data. If the DBMS provides a way to interactively enter and update the database, as well as interrogate it, this capability allows for managing personal databases.</p>

<p id="mwAtU"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_security" title="Data security" id="mwAtY">Data security</a> in general deals with protecting specific chunks of data, both physically (i.e., from corruption, or destruction, or removal; e.g., see <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_security" title="Physical security" id="mwAtc">physical security</a>), or the interpretation of them, or parts of them to meaningful information (e.g., by looking at the strings of bits that they comprise, concluding specific valid credit-card numbers; e.g., see <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_encryption" title="Data encryption" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAtg">data encryption</a>).</p>

<p id="mwAtk">Change and access logging records who accessed which attributes, what was changed, and when it was changed. Logging services allow for a forensic <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_audit" title="Database audit" id="mwAto">database audit</a> later by keeping a record of access occurrences and changes. Sometimes application-level code is used to record changes rather than leaving this in the database. Monitoring can be set up to attempt to detect security breaches. Therefore, organizations must take database security seriously because of the many benefits it provides. Organizations will be safeguarded from security breaches and hacking activities like firewall intrusion, virus spread, and ransom ware. This helps in protecting the company's essential information, which cannot be shared with outsiders at any cause.<sup about="#mwt171" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-41" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-41"}}'><a href="#cite_note-41" id="mwAts"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAtw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAt0">[</span>40<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAt4">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="21" id="mwAt8" aria-labelledby="Transactions_and_concurrency"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Transactions_and_concurrency">Transactions and concurrency</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt172" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAuA" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Further","href":"./Template:Further"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Concurrency control"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt173" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt172" id="mwAuE">Further information: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concurrency_control" title="Concurrency control">Concurrency control</a></div>

<p id="mwAuI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_transactions" title="Database transactions" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAuM">Database transactions</a> can be used to introduce some level of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fault_tolerance" title="Fault tolerance" id="mwAuQ">fault tolerance</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_integrity" title="Data integrity" id="mwAuU">data integrity</a> after recovery from a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crash_(computing)" title="Crash (computing)" id="mwAuY">crash</a>. A database transaction is a unit of work, typically encapsulating a number of operations over a database (e.g., reading a database object, writing, acquiring or releasing a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lock_(database)" title="Lock (database)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAuc">lock</a>, etc.), an abstraction supported in database and also other systems. Each transaction has well defined boundaries in terms of which program/code executions are included in that transaction (determined by the transaction's programmer via special transaction commands).</p>

<p id="mwAug">The acronym <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACID" title="ACID" id="mwAuk">ACID</a> describes some ideal properties of a database transaction: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomicity_(database_systems)" title="Atomicity (database systems)" id="mwAuo">atomicity</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consistency_(database_systems)" title="Consistency (database systems)" id="mwAus">consistency</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolation_(database_systems)" title="Isolation (database systems)" id="mwAuw">isolation</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durability_(database_systems)" title="Durability (database systems)" id="mwAu0">durability</a>.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="22" id="mwAu4" aria-labelledby="Migration"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Migration">Migration</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt174" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAu8" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"See also","href":"./Template:See_also"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Data migration#Database migration"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt175" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt174" id="mwAvA">See also: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_migration#Database_migration" title="Data migration">Data migration §<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>Database migration</a></div>

<p id="mwAvE">A database built with one DBMS is not portable to another DBMS (i.e., the other DBMS cannot run it). However, in some situations, it is desirable to migrate a database from one DBMS to another. The reasons are primarily economical (different DBMSs may have different <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_cost_of_ownership" title="Total cost of ownership" id="mwAvI">total costs of ownership</a> or TCOs), functional, and operational (different DBMSs may have different capabilities). The migration involves the database's transformation from one DBMS type to another. The transformation should maintain (if possible) the database related application (i.e., all related application programs) intact. Thus, the database's conceptual and external architectural levels should be maintained in the transformation. It may be desired that also some aspects of the architecture internal level are maintained. A complex or large database migration may be a complicated and costly (one-time) project by itself, which should be factored into the decision to migrate. This is in spite of the fact that tools may exist to help migration between specific DBMSs. Typically, a DBMS vendor provides tools to help import databases from other popular DBMSs.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="23" id="mwAvM" aria-labelledby="Building,_maintaining,_and_tuning"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Building,_maintaining,_and_tuning"><span id="Building.2C_maintaining.2C_and_tuning" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Building, maintaining, and tuning</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt176" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAvQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Database tuning"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt177" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt176" id="mwAvU">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_tuning" title="Database tuning">Database tuning</a></div>

<p id="mwAvY">After designing a database for an application, the next stage is building the database. Typically, an appropriate general-purpose DBMS can be selected to be used for this purpose. A DBMS provides the needed <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/User_interface" title="User interface" id="mwAvc">user interfaces</a> to be used by database administrators to define the needed application's data structures within the DBMS's respective data model. Other user interfaces are used to select needed DBMS parameters (like security related, storage allocation parameters, etc.).</p>

<p id="mwAvg">When the database is ready (all its data structures and other needed components are defined), it is typically populated with initial application's data (database initialization, which is typically a distinct project; in many cases using specialized DBMS interfaces that support bulk insertion) before making it operational. In some cases, the database becomes operational while empty of application data, and data are accumulated during its operation.</p>

<p id="mwAvk">After the database is created, initialized and populated it needs to be maintained. Various database parameters may need changing and the database may need to be tuned (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_tuning" title="Database tuning" id="mwAvo">tuning</a>) for better performance; application's data structures may be changed or added, new related application programs may be written to add to the application's functionality, etc.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="24" id="mwAvs" aria-labelledby="Backup_and_restore"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Backup_and_restore">Backup and restore</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt178" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAvw" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Backup"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt179" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt178" id="mwAv0">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backup" title="Backup">Backup</a></div>
<p id="mwAv4">Sometimes it is desired to bring a database back to a previous state (for many reasons, e.g., cases when the database is found corrupted due to a software error, or if it has been updated with erroneous data). To achieve this, a backup operation is done occasionally or continuously, where each desired database state (i.e., the values of its data and their embedding in database's data structures) is kept within dedicated backup files (many techniques exist to do this effectively). When it is decided by a database administrator to bring the database back to this state (e.g., by specifying this state by a desired point in time when the database was in this state), these files are used to restore that state.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="25" id="mwAv8" aria-labelledby="Static_analysis"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Static_analysis">Static analysis</h2></div>
<p id="mwAwA">Static analysis techniques for software verification can be applied also in the scenario of query languages. In particular, the *<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_interpretation" title="Abstract interpretation" id="mwAwE">Abstract interpretation</a> framework has been extended to the field of query languages for relational databases as a way to support sound approximation techniques.<sup about="#mwt180" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalderCortesi2011_42-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEHalderCortesi2011"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalderCortesi2011-42"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"sfn","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Halder"},"2":{"wt":"Cortesi"},"3":{"wt":"2011"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalderCortesi2011-42" id="mwAwI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAwM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwQ">[</span>41<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwU">]</span></span></a></sup> The semantics of query languages can be tuned according to suitable abstractions of the concrete domain of data. The abstraction of relational database systems has many interesting applications, in particular, for security purposes, such as fine-grained access control, watermarking, etc.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="26" id="mwAwY" aria-labelledby="Miscellaneous_features"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Miscellaneous_features">Miscellaneous features</h2></div>

<p id="mwAwc">Other DBMS features might include:</p>

<ul id="mwAwg"><li id="mwAwk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_log" title="Database log" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAwo">Database logs</a> – This helps in keeping a history of the executed functions.</li>
<li id="mwAws">Graphics component for producing graphs and charts, especially in a data warehouse system.</li>
<li id="mwAww"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Query_optimizer" title="Query optimizer" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAw0">Query optimizer</a> – Performs query optimization on every query to choose an efficient <i id="mwAw4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Query_plan" title="Query plan" id="mwAw8">query plan</a></i> (a partial order (tree) of operations) to be executed to compute the query result. May be specific to a particular storage engine.</li>
<li id="mwAxA">Tools or hooks for database design, application programming, application program maintenance, database performance analysis and monitoring, database configuration monitoring, DBMS hardware configuration (a DBMS and related database may span computers, networks, and storage units) and related database mapping (especially for a distributed DBMS), storage allocation and database layout monitoring, storage migration, etc.</li></ul>

<p id="mwAxE">Increasingly, there are calls for a single system that incorporates all of these core functionalities into the same build, test, and deployment framework for database management and source control. Borrowing from other developments in the software industry, some market such offerings as "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DevOps" title="DevOps" id="mwAxI">DevOps</a> for database".<sup about="#mwt185" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-43" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-43"}}'><a href="#cite_note-43" id="mwAxM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAxQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxU">[</span>42<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxY">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="27" id="mwAxc" aria-labelledby="Design_and_modeling"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Design_and_modeling">Design and modeling</h2></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt186" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAxg" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Database design"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt187" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt186" id="mwAxk">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_design" title="Database design">Database design</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAxo"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Process_of_database_design_v2.png" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAxs"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Process_of_database_design_v2.png" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Process_of_database_design_v2.png/500px-Process_of_database_design_v2.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Process_of_database_design_v2.png/1280px-Process_of_database_design_v2.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="2656" data-file-height="1760" data-file-type="bitmap" height="331" width="500" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 2" id="mwAxw"/></a><figcaption id="mwAx0"></figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwAx4">The first task of a database designer is to produce a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conceptual_data_model" title="Conceptual data model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAx8">conceptual data model</a> that reflects the structure of the information to be held in the database. A common approach to this is to develop an <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entity–relationship_model" title="Entity–relationship model" id="mwAyA">entity–relationship model</a>, often with the aid of drawing tools. Another popular approach is the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_Modeling_Language" title="Unified Modeling Language" id="mwAyE">Unified Modeling Language</a>. A successful data model will accurately reflect the possible state of the external world being modeled: for example, if people can have more than one phone number, it will allow this information to be captured. Designing a good conceptual data model requires a good understanding of the application domain; it typically involves asking deep questions about the things of interest to an organization, like "can a customer also be a supplier?", or "if a product is sold with two different forms of packaging, are those the same product or different products?", or "if a plane flies from New York to Dubai via Frankfurt, is that one flight or two (or maybe even three)?". The answers to these questions establish definitions of the terminology used for entities (customers, products, flights, flight segments) and their relationships and attributes.</p>

<p id="mwAyI">Producing the conceptual data model sometimes involves input from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_process_modeling" title="Business process modeling" id="mwAyM">business processes</a>, or the analysis of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workflow" title="Workflow" id="mwAyQ">workflow</a> in the organization. This can help to establish what information is needed in the database, and what can be left out. For example, it can help when deciding whether the database needs to hold historic data as well as current data.</p>

<p id="mwAyU">Having produced a conceptual data model that users are happy with, the next stage is to translate this into a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_schema" title="Database schema" id="mwAyY">schema</a> that implements the relevant data structures within the database. This process is often called logical database design, and the output is a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logical_data_model" title="Logical data model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAyc">logical data model</a> expressed in the form of a schema. Whereas the conceptual data model is (in theory at least) independent of the choice of database technology, the logical data model will be expressed in terms of a particular database model supported by the chosen DBMS. (The terms <i id="mwAyg">data model</i> and <i id="mwAyk">database model</i> are often used interchangeably, but in this article we use <i id="mwAyo">data model</i> for the design of a specific database, and <i id="mwAys">database model</i> for the modeling notation used to express that design).</p>

<p id="mwAyw">The most popular database model for general-purpose databases is the relational model, or more precisely, the relational model as represented by the SQL language. The process of creating a logical database design using this model uses a methodical approach known as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_normalization" title="Database normalization" id="mwAy0">normalization</a>. The goal of normalization is to ensure that each elementary "fact" is only recorded in one place, so that insertions, updates, and deletions automatically maintain consistency.</p>

<p id="mwAy4">The final stage of database design is to make the decisions that affect performance, scalability, recovery, security, and the like, which depend on the particular DBMS. This is often called <i id="mwAy8">physical database design</i>, and the output is the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_data_model" title="Physical data model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAzA">physical data model</a>. A key goal during this stage is <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_independence" title="Data independence" id="mwAzE">data independence</a>, meaning that the decisions made for performance optimization purposes should be invisible to end-users and applications. There are two types of data independence: Physical data independence and logical data independence. Physical design is driven mainly by performance requirements, and requires a good knowledge of the expected workload and access patterns, and a deep understanding of the features offered by the chosen DBMS.</p>

<p id="mwAzI">Another aspect of physical database design is security. It involves both defining <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Access_control" title="Access control" id="mwAzM">access control</a> to database objects as well as defining security levels and methods for the data itself.</p>

<section data-mw-section-id="28" id="mwAzQ" aria-labelledby="Models"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Models">Models</h3></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt188" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAzU" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Main","href":"./Template:Main"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Database model"}},"i":0}}]}'><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt189" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw='{"name":"templatestyles","attrs":{"src":"Module:Hatnote/styles.css"},"body":{"extsrc":""}}'/></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt188" id="mwAzY">Main article: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database_model" title="Database model">Database model</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAzc"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Database_models.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAzg"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Database_models.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Database_models.jpg/500px-Database_models.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Database_models.jpg/1280px-Database_models.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="1341" data-file-height="965" data-file-type="bitmap" height="360" width="500" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 2" id="mwAzk"/></a><figcaption id="mwAzo">Collage of five types of database models</figcaption></figure>

<p id="mwAzs">A database model is a type of data model that determines the logical structure of a database and fundamentally determines in which manner <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data" title="Data" id="mwAzw">data</a> can be stored, organized, and manipulated. The most popular example of a database model is the relational model (or the SQL approximation of relational), which uses a table-based format.</p>

<p id="mwAz0">Common logical data models for databases include:</p>
<ul id="mwAz4"><li id="mwAz8"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navigational_database" title="Navigational database" id="mwA0A">Navigational databases</a>
<ul id="mwA0E"><li id="mwA0I"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchical_database_model" title="Hierarchical database model" id="mwA0M">Hierarchical database model</a></li>
<li id="mwA0Q"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_model" title="Network model" id="mwA0U">Network model</a></li>
<li id="mwA0Y"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graph_database" title="Graph database" id="mwA0c">Graph database</a></li></ul></li>
<li id="mwA0g"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relational_model" title="Relational model" id="mwA0k">Relational model</a></li>
<li id="mwA0o"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entity–relationship_model" title="Entity–relationship model" id="mwA0s">Entity–relationship model</a>
<ul id="mwA0w"><li id="mwA00"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_entity–relationship_model" title="Enhanced entity–relationship model" id="mwA04">Enhanced entity–relationship model</a></li></ul></li>
<li id="mwA08"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Object_database" title="Object database" id="mwA1A">Object model</a></li>
<li id="mwA1E"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Document-oriented_database" title="Document-oriented database" id="mwA1I">Document model</a></li>
<li id="mwA1M"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entity–attribute–value_model" title="Entity–attribute–value model" id="mwA1Q">Entity–attribute–value model</a></li>
<li id="mwA1U"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_schema" title="Star schema" id="mwA1Y">Star schema</a></li></ul>

<p id="mwA1c">An object–relational database combines the two related structures.</p>

<p id="mwA1g"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_data_model" title="Physical data model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA1k">Physical data models</a> include:</p>
<ul id="mwA1o"><li id="mwA1s"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverted_index" title="Inverted index" id="mwA1w">Inverted index</a></li>
<li id="mwA10"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_file" title="Flat file" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA14">Flat file</a></li></ul>

<p id="mwA18">Other models include:</p>
<ul id="mwA2A"><li id="mwA2E"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multidimensional_database" title="Multidimensional database" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA2I">Multidimensional model</a></li>
<li id="mwA2M"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Array_DBMS" title="Array DBMS" id="mwA2Q">Array model</a></li>
<li id="mwA2U"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multivalue_model" title="Multivalue model" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA2Y">Multivalue model</a></li></ul>

<p id="mwA2c">Specialized models are optimized for particular types of data:</p>
<ul id="mwA2g"><li id="mwA2k"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XML_database" title="XML database" id="mwA2o">XML database</a></li>
<li id="mwA2s"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantic_data_model" title="Semantic data model" id="mwA2w">Semantic model</a></li>
<li id="mwA20"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Content_store" title="Content store" class="mw-redirect" id="mwA24">Content store</a></li>
<li id="mwA28"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Event_store" title="Event store" id="mwA3A">Event store</a></li>
<li id="mwA3E"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_series_database" title="Time series database" id="mwA3I">Time series model</a></li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="29" id="mwA3M" aria-labelledby="External,_conceptual,_and_internal_views"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="External,_conceptual,_and_internal_views"><span id="External.2C_conceptual.2C_and_internal_views" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>External, conceptual, and internal views</h3></div>

<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwA3Q"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Traditional_View_of_Data_SVG.svg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwA3U"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Traditional_View_of_Data_SVG.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Traditional_View_of_Data_SVG.svg/330px-Traditional_View_of_Data_SVG.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/Traditional_View_of_Data_SVG.svg/960px-Traditional_View_of_Data_SVG.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="433" data-file-height="321" data-file-type="drawing" height="215" width="290" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 1.15" id="mwA3Y"/></a><figcaption id="mwA3c">Traditional view of data<sup about="#mwt191" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-ITL93_44-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"ITL93"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-ITL93-44"}}'><a href="#cite_note-ITL93-44" id="mwA3g"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA3k"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3o">[</span>43<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA3s">]</span></span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>

<p id="mwA3w">A database management system provides three views of the database data:</p>

<ul id="mwA30"><li id="mwA34">The <b id="mwA38">external level</b> defines how each group of end-users sees the organization of data in the database. A single database can have any number of views at the external level.</li>
